Well-Posedness for 2D Combustion Model in Bounded Domains and Serrin-Type Blowup Criterion
Jiawen Zhang

Abstract
We investigate the 2D combustion model with Dirichlet boundary conditions and slip boundary conditions in bounded domains. The global existence of weak and strong solutions and the uniqueness of strong solutions are obtained provided the initial density is small in some precise sense. Using the energy method and the estimates of boundary integrals, we obtain the a priori bounds of the density and velocity field. In addition, we prove the local existence of the strong solutions via iterative method and the contraction mapping theorem. Finally, we extend the well-known Serrin’s blowup criterion to the 2D combustion model. Under the suitable boundary conditions, the Serrin’s condition on the velocity can be removed in this criteria. Keywords: combustion model; Dirichlet boundary conditions; slip boundary conditions; strong solutions; weak solutions; Serrin’s condition



1
Intrduction and main results

In this paper, we will study the following combustion model:

(1.1)

\begin{cases}\rho_{t}+\dive(\rho u)=0,\,\,\rho\geq 0,\\
(\rho u)_{t}+\dive(\rho u\otimes u)-\dive(2\mu D)+\nabla\pi=0,\\
\dive u=c_{0}\Delta\psi(\rho),\,\,\psi(\rho):=\rho^{-1},\end{cases}

for
t>0
and
x\in\Omega
, where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a bounded simply connected domain with smooth boundary. Here
u=(u_{1},u_{2})
,
\rho
and
\pi
stand for the unknown velocity field, density and pressure respectively,
c_{0}>0
is a fixed constant and

0<\mu=\mu(s)\in C^{\infty}[0,\infty).

We denote

D=D(u)=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla u+(\nabla u)^{t})=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{i}u_{j}+\partial_{j}u_{i}),

for
1\leq i,j\leq 2
.

From the physical viewpoint, combustion model is the low Mach number limit of the fully compressible Navier-Stokes equations

(FCNS)

\begin{cases}\rho_{t}+\dive(\rho u)=0,\\
(\rho u)_{t}+\dive(\rho u\otimes u)-\dive\mathbb{S}+\nabla p=0,\\
(\rho e)_{t}+\dive(\rho ue)-\dive(k\nabla\theta)+p\dive u=\mathbb{S}:D(u),\end{cases}

where
e,\,\theta,\,p
stands for the internal enery, temperature and pressure respectively and
A:B
strands for the inner product of matrices

A:B:=\text{tr}(AB^{t})=\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}a_{ij}b_{ji}.

\mathbb{S}
is the viscous strain tensor given by

\mathbb{S}=2\mu D(u)+\lambda\dive uI_{n\times n},

where
I_{n\times n}
is the
n\times n
indentity matrix. The thermal conductivity
k
and the viscosity coefficients
\mu,\,\lambda
are functions of
\rho
and
\theta
. From Lions’s book
[
27
]
, if we define the Mach number
\epsilon
as
|u|/\sqrt{p^{\prime}(\rho)}
and let
(\rho,u,\theta)
be smooth solution of system (
FCNS
) corresponding to the small
\epsilon
, after rescaling the time variable by

\rho^{\epsilon}(x,t)=\rho\left(x,\frac{t}{\epsilon}\right),\quad u^{\epsilon}(x,t)=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\rho\left(x,\frac{t}{\epsilon}\right),\quad\theta^{\epsilon}(x,t)=\theta\left(x,\frac{t}{\epsilon}\right),

then,
(\rho^{\epsilon},u^{\epsilon},\theta^{\epsilon})
satisfies

(FCNS’)

\begin{cases}\rho^{\epsilon}_{t}+\dive(\rho^{\epsilon}u^{\epsilon})=0,\\
(\rho^{\epsilon}u^{\epsilon})_{t}+\dive(\rho u^{\epsilon}\otimes u^{\epsilon})-\dive\mathbb{S}^{\epsilon}+\epsilon^{-2}\nabla p^{\epsilon}=0,\\
(\rho^{\epsilon}e^{\epsilon})_{t}+\dive(\rho^{\epsilon}u^{\epsilon}e^{\epsilon})-\dive(k^{\epsilon}\nabla\theta^{\epsilon})+p^{\epsilon}\dive u^{\epsilon}=\epsilon^{2}\mathbb{S}^{\epsilon}\cdot D(u^{\epsilon}),\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{\epsilon}=2\mu^{\epsilon}D(u^{\epsilon})+\lambda^{\epsilon}\operatorname{div}u^{\epsilon}I_{n\times n},

and

\displaystyle p^{\epsilon}=p\left(x,\frac{t}{\epsilon}\right),\quad\mu^{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mu\left(x,\frac{t}{\epsilon}\right),\quad\lambda^{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\lambda\left(x,\frac{t}{\epsilon}\right),\quad k^{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}k\left(x,\frac{t}{\epsilon}\right).

Considerig the ideal gas laws:

(1.2)

p=R\rho\theta,\quad e=C_{V}\theta,

where
R,\,C_{V}
denote the ideal gas constant and the specific heat constant, respectively. Then, letting the Mach number
\epsilon
go to 0, the momentum equation
\eqref{FCNS2}_{2}
implies that

p^{\epsilon}=P(t)+\pi(t,x)\epsilon^{2}+o\left(\epsilon^{2}\right).

Plugging this formula into the energy equation
\eqref{FCNS2}_{3}
entails that
P(t)
is independent of
t
, provided
u^{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\theta^{\epsilon}
vanish at infinity. From now on, we shall denote this constant by
P_{0}
. Therefore, denoting
C_{P}=\gamma C_{V}=\gamma R/(\gamma-1)
, the low Mach number limit system reads

(1.3)

\begin{cases}\rho C_{P}\left(\partial_{t}\theta+u\cdot\nabla\theta\right)-\operatorname{div}(k\nabla\theta)=0,\\
\rho u_{t}+\rho u\cdot\nabla u-\operatorname{div}\mathbb{S}+\nabla\pi=0,\\
\gamma P_{0}\operatorname{div}u=(\gamma-1)\operatorname{div}(k\nabla\theta).\end{cases}

Plugging (
1.2
) into (
1.3
) with constant heat conductivity coefficient
k
implies the following system

(1.4)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\dive(\rho u)=0,\\
\rho u_{t}+\rho u\cdot\nabla u-\operatorname{div}\mathbb{S}+\nabla\pi=0,\\
\dive u={k(\gamma-1)}(R\gamma)^{-1}\Delta\rho^{-1},\end{cases}

which is exactly the equations (
1.1
).

If we particularly take the diffusion coefficient
c_{0}=0
, (
1.1
) will become the classical non-homogeneous incompressible Navier-Stokes equations

(N)

\begin{cases}\rho_{t}+\dive(\rho u)=0,\,\,\rho\geq 0,\\
(\rho u)_{t}+\dive(\rho u\otimes u)-\dive(2\mu D)+\nabla\pi=0,\\
\dive u=0.\end{cases}

The study of the combustion model may date back to the 1980s. It has been introduced by A. Majda
[
29
]
and studied in particular by P. Embid
[
14
]
who has proved the local-in-time well-posedness of the system (
1.1
). For the system (
1.1
) replacing
\eqref{equation1.1}_{3}
by Fick’s law with
\psi(\rho)=\log\rho
, the local well-posedness was considered by H. B. da Veiga
[
12
]
. Danchin-Liao
[
13
]
established the local existence and uniqueness of a solution in critical homogeneous Besov spaces provided the density is closed to a positive constant and they proved the local well-posedness in non-homogeneous Besov space arbitrarily large data.

On the other hand, there are also a large number of works investigating the global-in-time existence of weak and strong solutions for the combustion model. P. Secchi
[
32
]
proved that there exists a unique global strong solution in the two-dimensional domain under Fick’s law providing the diffusion coefficient
c_{0}
is small enough. They also considered the limiting behavior of the solutions when
c_{0}\to 0
for dimensions 2 and 3 and the convergence towards the corresponding solutions of (
N
). Under the small initial data assumption, P. Lions
[
28
]
showed, in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
or periodic boundary condition, that a small perturbation of a constant density gives a global existence of weak solutions without any restriction on the initial velocity. Danchin-Liao
[
13
]
proved the existence of solutions in critical homogeneous Besov spaces by assuming the initial density is close to a constant and the initial velocity is small enough. Recently, W. Tan
[
37
]
proved the global existence of the weak and strong solutions of the system (
1.1
) with general viscosity coefficient
\mu(\rho)
in
\eqref{equation1.1}_{2}
and
\psi(\rho)
in
\eqref{equation1.1}_{3}
provided the density is closed to a positive constant in some precise sense. For large data, Bresch-Essoufi-Sy
[
5
]
showed the global existence of the weak solutions for the combustion model in dimensions 2 and 3 by taking
\mu(\rho)=\frac{c_{0}}{2}\log\rho
. In
[
6
]
, Bresch-Giovangigli-Zatorska relaxed the restriction on
\mu(\rho)
by using the idea of the renormalized solution.

If one takes the decomposition
u=v+c_{0}\nabla\rho^{-1}
with
\dive v=0
and converts the system (
1.1
) to the equations for
(\rho,v)
, then (
1.1
) will be reduced to the Kazhikhov-Smagulov type model, see (
1.18
). In
[
9
,
10
]
, Cai-Liao-Sun established the global-in-time existence of strong solutions to the initial-boundary value problem of a 2D Kazhikhov-Smagulov type model for incompressible non-homogeneous fluids with mass diffusion for the arbitrary size of initial data. For works on the classical Kazhikhov-Smagulov’s model, we refer the reader to
[
2
,
4
]
.

For the general non-homogeneous incompressible Navier-Stokes equations (
N
) with the viscosity coefficient
\mu(\rho)
depending on
\rho
, global weak solutions were derived by P. Lions
[
27
]
. Abidi-Zhang
[
1
]
obtained the global strong solutions strictly away from vacuum whenever
\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\nabla u_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}
and
\left\|\mu(\rho_{0})-1\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
are small enough. For the initial density containing vacuum, Cho-Kim
[
11
]
established the existence of the local strong solutions under compatibility conditions similar to
[
23
]
. In addition, Huang-Wang
[
22
]
, J. Zhang
[
40
]
established the global strong solutions with small
\left\|\nabla u_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}
in 3D bounded domains. For the Cauchy problem, He-Li-L
\mathrm{\ddot{u}}

[
18
]
obtained the global strong ones to with small
\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{\beta}}
for some
\beta\in(1/2,1]
and some extra restrictions on
\mu(\rho)
via the exponential decay-in-time estimates. More recently, Cai-L
\mathrm{\ddot{u}}
-Peng
[
8
]
studied the global existence of strong solutions in 3D exterior domains with nonslip or slip boundary conditions provided that the gradient of the initial velocity is suitably small.

Finally, for the study of the mechanism of blowup and structure of possible singularities of strong (or smooth) solutions to the Navier-Stokes system can be traced to Serrin’s criterion
[
33
]
on the Leray-Hopf weak solutions to the 3D incompressible homogeneous Navier-Stokes equations, which can showed that if a weak solution
u
satisfies

(1.5)

u\in L^{s}(0,T;L^{r}),\quad\frac{2}{s}+\frac{3}{r}\leq 1,\quad 3<r\leq\infty,

then it is regular. Later, He-Xin
[
19
]
showed that the Serrin’s criterion (
1.5
) still holds even for the strong solution to the incompressible MHD equations. For non-homogeneous incompressible Navier–Stokes equations (
N
), H. Kim
[
24
]
established the Serrin-type blowup criterion. They showed that if
(\rho,u)
blows up at
T^{*}
, then

(1.6)

\lim_{t\to T^{*}}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{s}(0,T;L^{r}_{w})}=\infty\quad\text{for all}\quad\frac{2}{s}+\frac{3}{r}\leq 1,\quad 3<r\leq\infty.

Recently, X. Zhong
[
41
]
obtained a blowup criterion (
1.5
) to the non-homogeneous incompressible heat conducting Navier–Stokes flows with non-negative density in bounded domain of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For the compressible fluids, Huang-Li-Xin
[
21
]
first extend Serrin’s blow-up criterion to the barotropic compressible Navier-Stokes equations. Later, Xu-Zhang
[
39
]
extended the results of
[
21
]
to the isentropic compressible MHD system
and Huang-Li-Wang
[
20
]
improve the all previous blowup criterion results to the full compressible Navier–Stokes system.

However, for the general viscosity coefficient, the theory of the combustion model in the bounded domain is still blank. Therefore, our goal is obtaining the global existence of solutions with small initial data and the local existence for (
1.1
) in the general domain under different initial-boundary conditions and trying to extend Serrin’s blow-up criterion to (
1.1
).

More precisely, we impose the initial data

(1.7)

u_{0}(x):=u(x,0),\quad 0<\alpha\leq\rho_{0}(x):=\rho(x,0)\leq\beta<\infty,\quad x\in\Omega

and one of the following boundary conditions:

(1)

\rho
satisfies the Neumann condition and
u
satisfies the slip boundary condition, that is,

(A)

n\cdot\nabla\rho=0,\quad u\cdot n=0\text{ and }\curle u=-n^{\perp}\cdot B\cdot u\quad\mathrm{on}\,\,\partial\Omega\times(0,T),

where
n=(n_{1},n_{2})
denotes the unit outer normal vector of the boundary
\partial\Omega
,
n^{\perp}=(n_{2},-n_{1})
is the unit tangential vector on the boundary and
B=B(x)
is a bounded smooth symmetric matrix which is positive semi-definite;

(2)

(\rho,u)
satisfies the non-homogeneous Dirichlet condition, that is,

(B)

\rho=\tilde{\rho},\quad u=c_{0}\nabla\rho^{-1}\quad\mathrm{on}\,\,\partial\Omega\times(0,T),

where
\tilde{\rho}
is a positive constant such that
\alpha\leq\tilde{\rho}\leq\beta
;

(3)

\rho
satisfies the Neumann condition and
u
satisfies the non-slip condition, that is,

(C)

n\cdot\nabla\rho=0,\quad u=0\quad\mathrm{on}\,\,\partial\Omega\times(0,T).

Before giving the main results, we explain some notations and conventions used throughout the paper. For simplicity, we set

\int f:=\int_{\Omega}f\,dx,\,\,\,\int_{\partial}f:=\int_{\partial\Omega}f\,dS,\,\,\,\iint f:=\iint_{Q_{T}}f\,dxdt,

where
Q_{T}:=\Omega\times(0,T)
, and

f_{\Omega}:=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int f,

where
|E|
stands for the Lebesgue measure of the measurable set
E
.

Also, for all integer
k
and
1\leq p<\infty
,
W^{k,p}
is the standard Sobolev spaces as defined as follows:

\begin{cases}L^{p}:=L^{p}(\Omega),\,\,W^{k,p}=W^{k,p}(\Omega),\\
H^{k}:=W^{k,2},\,\,H^{\infty}:=\bigcap_{k\geq 1}H^{k},\\
W^{k,p}_{0}=\overline{C_{0}^{\infty}}\,\,\mathrm{closure\,\,in\,\,the\,\,norm\,\,of\,\,}W^{k,p},\\
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{B_{1}\cap B_{2}}:=\left\|\cdot\right\|_{B_{1}}+\left\|\cdot\right\|_{B_{2}}\text{ for two Banach spaces }B_{1}\text{ and }B_{2},\\
H^{1}_{\omega}:=\{u\in H^{1}:u\cdot n=0,\,\curle u=-n^{\perp}\cdot B\cdot u\text{ on }\partial\Omega\},\\
H^{1}_{nd}:=\{u\in H^{1}:u=c_{0}\nabla\rho^{-1}\text{ on }\partial\Omega\},\\
V^{0,2}:=\{u\in L^{2}:\dive u=0,\,u\cdot n=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega\},\\
V_{0}^{1,2}:=\{u\in H^{1}_{0}:\dive u=0\},\quad V_{0}^{-1,2}:=[V_{0}^{1,2}]^{*}.\end{cases}

For
0<\gamma<1
, we denote by
C^{\gamma}(\overline{\Omega})
the standard H
\mathrm{\ddot{o}}
lder space and
\rho\in C^{\gamma,\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\overline{Q}_{T})
the parabolic one, that is,

C^{\gamma,\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\overline{Q}_{T}):=\left\{f\in C(\overline{Q}_{T}):\sup_{(x,t),(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in\overline{Q}_{T}\atop(x,t)\neq(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})}\frac{|f(x,t)-f(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})|}{|x-x^{\prime}|^{\gamma}+|t-t^{\prime}|^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}}<\infty\right\}.

The weak, weak* and strong convergence of a sequence
\{f^{n}\}
are respectively denoted by

f^{n}\xrightharpoonup{\,\,w\,}f,\quad f^{n}\xrightharpoonup{w^{*}}f,\quad f^{n}\xrightarrow{\,\,s\,\,}f.

Finally, the transpose gradient is given by

\displaystyle\nabla^{\perp}:=(\partial_{2},-\partial_{1}).

With this notion, one can write

\begin{cases}\curle u=\nabla^{\perp}\cdot u,\\
\Delta u=\nabla\dive u+\nabla^{\perp}\curle u.\end{cases}

Now, we give the definitions of weak solutions and strong ones.

Definition 1.1

(Weak Solutions)
.

(\rho,u)
is called a global weak solution, if the following regularity properties hold:

(1.8)

\begin{cases}\alpha\leq\rho\leq\beta,\\
\rho\in C([0,T];H^{1})\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}),\quad\rho_{t}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}),\\
\begin{cases}u\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2})\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}_{\omega}),\quad\,\,\text{case }\eqref{A},\\
u\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2})\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}_{nd}),\quad\text{case }\eqref{B},\end{cases}\end{cases}

and
(\rho,u)
statisfies (
1.1
) in the sense of distributions for all
T\in(0,\infty)
. More precisely,
\eqref{equation1.1}_{1}
,
\eqref{equation1.1}_{3}
hold almost everywhere in
\Omega\times(0,T)
and
\eqref{equation1.1}_{2}
is satisfied in the following sense:

(1.9)

\displaystyle\iint\rho u\cdot\phi_{t}+\rho u\otimes u:\nabla\phi-2\mu D(u):D(\phi)=-\int\rho_{0}u_{0}\cdot\phi(x,0),

for
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\overline{Q}_{T})
with
\dive\phi=0
,
\phi(x,T)=0
,
x\in\Omega
and
\phi=0
on
\partial\Omega\times(0,T)
.

Definition 1.2

(Strong Solutions)
.

If
(\rho,u,\pi)
is a solution such that (
1.1
) holds almost everywhere in
\Omega\times(0,T)
,
T\in(0,\infty)
, such that

(1.10)

\begin{cases}\alpha\leq\rho\leq\beta,\\
\rho\in C([0,T];H^{2})\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{3}),\\
\rho_{t}\in C([0,T];L^{2})\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}),\\
u\in C([0,T];H^{1})\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}),\\
u_{t}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}),\\
\pi\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}),\end{cases}

we call
(\rho,u,\pi)
the strong solution on
\Omega\times(0,T)
. In particular, if
(\rho,u,\pi)
satisfies (
1.10
) for all
T\in(0,\infty)
, we say that
(\rho,u,\pi)
is a global strong solution of the system (
1.1
).

Our main results sate as following. The first two theorems concern with the existence results for
(\rho,u)
satisfying (
A
) or (
B
).

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose that
u_{0}\in L^{2}
and
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfies the following compatibility condition

(1.11)

\begin{cases}\dive u_{0}=c_{0}\Delta\rho_{0}^{-1},&x\in\Omega\\
u_{0}\cdot n=n\cdot\nabla\rho_{0}^{-1},&x\in\partial\Omega\end{cases}

Assume that
(\rho,u)
satisfies the condition
(
A
)
or
(
B
)
, then there exist a positive constant
\delta
which only depends on
\Omega
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
c_{0}
and
\left\|v_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}
such that, if
\left\|\nabla\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq\delta
, problem (
1.1
) and (
1.7
) admits at least one gobal weak solution.

Theorem 1.4
.

Suppose that
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfies (
1.11
) and
(\rho,u)
satisfies the condition
(
A
)
or
(
B
)
. Let
u_{0}\in H^{1}_{\omega}
provided
u
satisfying the condition
(
A
)
;
u_{0}\in H^{1}_{nd}
provided
u
satisfying the condition
(
B
)
. In addition, let
\pi
satisfy the normalized condition

(1.12)

\int\pi=0.

Then, if
\left\|\nabla\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq\delta
with the same
\delta
obtained in Theorem
1.3
, the problem (
1.1
) and (
1.7
) admits a unique global strong solution
(\rho,u,\pi)
.

Next, for the case when
(\rho,u)
satisfying the condition (
C
), we have

Theorem 1.5
.

Suppose that
u_{0}\in H^{1}_{0}
and
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfies (
1.11
). Suppose that
(\rho,u)
satisfies the condition
(
C
)
and
\pi
satisfies (
1.12
). There exists a positive constant
\delta
which only depends on
\Omega
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
c_{0}
such that, if
\left\|\nabla u_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq\delta
, then the problem (
1.1
) and (
1.7
) admits a unique global strong solution
(\rho,u,\pi)
.

At last, we give the local existence result and the corresponding Serrin-type blowup criterion.

Theorem 1.6
.

Assume that
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfies (
1.11
) and
u_{0}\in H^{1}_{\omega},\,H^{1}_{nd}
provided
u
satisfying the condition (
A
) and (
B
), respectively. Let
\pi
saitisfies the condition (
1.12
). Then there exists a positive time
T_{1}<\infty
depending on
\Omega
,
c_{0}
,
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{H^{1}}
so that the problem (
1.1
) and (
1.7
) admits an unique strong solution
(\rho,u,\pi)
on
\Omega\times(0,T_{1})
.

Moreover, if
\mu(\rho)=\mu
is a positive constant and
u_{0}\in H^{1}_{0}
, then, the same result holds for
(\rho,u)
satisfying the condition (
C
)

Theorem 1.7
.

If
(\rho,u,\pi)
is a strong solution of (
1.1
) on
\Omega\times(0,T^{*})
and
T^{*}<\infty
is the maximal time of existence, then, one has

(1)

(1.13)

\lim_{T\to T^{*}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{s}(0,T;L^{r})}=\infty

provided
(\rho,u)
satisfying the condition (
A
) or (
B
);

(2)

(1.14)

\lim_{T\to T^{*}}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{s}(0,T;L^{r})}=\infty

provided
(\rho,u)
satisfying the condition (
C
).

Here,
r
and
s
satisfy the relation

(1.15)

\frac{2}{s}+\frac{2}{r}\leq 1,\quad 2<r\leq\infty.

Remark 1.8
.

The definition of
v_{0}
in Theorem
1.3
–
1.4
will be given at the end of this section.

Remark 1.9
.

Theorem
1.3
–
1.6
are first results concerning with the weak and strong solutions for the combustion model in bounded domain. Theorem
1.5
can be seen as a kind of extension of the global existence results in
[
22
,
40
]
with
\dive u=c_{0}\Delta\rho^{-1}
. Theorem
1.7
can be regarded as an extension to the classical Serrin’s condition.

Remark 1.10
.

For some technical reasons, in the proof of Theorem
1.4
, we need the following consistency condition

(1.16)

\rho_{0}|_{\partial\Omega}=\tilde{\rho}

to ensure the continuity of
\rho
, which is crucial to the higher order estimates of
v
, see subsection
3.3
for details. On the other hand, one should notice that the restriction
\alpha\leq\tilde{\rho}\leq\beta
and the condition (
1.16
) are not necessary for the proof of Theorem
1.3
. However, for simiplicity, we may always impose these requirements.

Remark 1.11
.

Noticing that, in Theorem
1.3
–
1.6
, we only impose the regularity restrictions on
u_{0}
for given initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
. This is due to the compatiability condition (
1.11
) from which one can find that the regularity of
\rho_{0}
can be totally determinded by that of
u_{0}
. Indeed, for example, if
u_{0}\in H^{1}_{0}
as we assumed in Theorem
1.5
, it follows from the following epllitic problem

\begin{cases}c_{0}\Delta\rho_{0}^{-1}=\dive u_{0},&x\in\Omega,\\
n\cdot\nabla\rho_{0}^{-1}=0,&x\in\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

that, for all
1<p<\infty
,

\begin{cases}\left\|\nabla\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{p}}\leq C(p)\left\|u_{0}\right\|_{L^{p}},\\
\left\|\nabla\rho_{0}\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\left\|\nabla u_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}.\end{cases}

Thus, alonging with the fact that
\rho_{0}\in L^{\infty}
,
\rho_{0}\in H^{2}
. We will come to this point again many times in later sections.

At the end of this section, we make a short comment on the analysis of this paper. Formally speaking, we treat Theorem
1.3
–
1.5
via two different types of decomposition and the proofs of Theorem
1.6
–
1.7
are based on those of Theorem
1.4
–
1.5
.

The proofs of Theorem
1.3
–
1.4
are based on the decomposition
u=v+c_{0}\nabla\rho^{-1}
, which may convert system (
1.1
) into the Kazhikhov-Smagulov type model. In this case, one can find that
v
satisfies either the Dirichlet boundary condition or the slip one. More precisely, we may first write in view of
\eqref{equation1.1}_{3}

(1.17)

v=u-c_{0}\nabla\rho^{-1}.

Of course, such
v
can be found for given
(\rho,u)
with the boundary condition (
A
) or (
B
). Next, using (
1.17
), we write

\rho u=\rho v+c_{0}\rho\nabla\rho^{-1}=\rho v-c_{0}\nabla\log\rho.

Therefore, combining this equality and (
1.17
), the original system (
1.1
) can be changed into the following equivalent formulations:

(1.18)

\begin{cases}\rho_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\rho+c_{0}\rho^{-2}\left|\nabla\rho\right|^{2}-c_{0}\rho^{-1}\Delta\rho=0,\\
\quad\\
\begin{cases}(\rho v)_{t}+\dive(\rho v\otimes v)-\dive{[2\mu D(v)]}+\nabla\pi_{1}=c_{0}\dive{\left(2\mu\nabla^{2}\rho^{-1}\right)}\\
-c_{0}\dive{\left(\rho v\otimes\nabla\rho^{-1}\right)}-\dive\left(c_{0}\rho\nabla\rho^{-1}\otimes v\right)-c_{0}^{2}\dive{\left(\rho\nabla\rho^{-1}\otimes\nabla\rho^{-1}\right)},\end{cases}\\
\quad\\
\dive{v}=0,\end{cases}

where
\pi_{1}=\pi-c_{0}(\log\rho)_{t}
is a modified pressure.

Next, we give a precise defintion for the initial-boundary value of
v
. For given initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfying the initial conditions (
1.11
), one can deduce that there exists a unique
v_{0}
satisfying

(1.19)

v(x,0):=v_{0}=u_{0}-c_{0}\nabla\rho_{0}^{-1},\quad\dive v_{0}=0,\quad v_{0}\cdot n=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega,

sharing with the similar compatibility conditions of
u_{0}
, that is,
v_{0}|_{\partial\Omega}=0
provided
u_{0}\in H^{1}_{nd}
and

\curle v_{0}=-n^{\perp}\cdot B\cdot(v_{0}+c_{0}\nabla\rho_{0}^{-1})

provided
u_{0}\in H^{1}_{\omega}
. Furthermore, from the relation (
1.17
), we can define the boundary conditions of
v
as follows:

(1)

v\cdot n=0
and
\curle v=\curle u=-n^{\perp}\cdot B\cdot(v+c_{0}\nabla\rho^{-1})
on
\partial\Omega\times(0,T)
, if
(\rho,u)
satisfies the condition (
A
). In this case, from Remark
2.6
in Section
2
, one has

\left\|v\right\|_{H^{2}}\leq C(\left\|\Delta v\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\Delta\rho^{-1}\right\|_{L^{2}}).

(2)

v=0
on
\partial\Omega\times(0,T)
, if
(\rho,u)
satisfies the condition (
B
). In this case, we have

\left\|v\right\|_{H^{2}}\leq C\left\|\Delta v\right\|_{L^{2}}.

An interesting observation is that, once the solution
(\rho,v)
of (
1.18
), which is defined as in Definition
1.1
, incorporating with the initial-boundary conditions given above, is established, one can expect to obtain
u
from (
1.17
) and, consequently,
(\rho,u)
becomes the solution of the original system (
1.1
). Therefore, in Section
3
, we mainly establish the a priori estimates of
(\rho,v)
. The details for proving the existence of
(\rho,u)
will be shown in Section
6
.

To sum up, we may impose
(\rho,v)
satisfying one of the following two boundary conditions

(1)

if
(\rho,u)
satisfies (
A
), we impose

(A’)

n\cdot\nabla\rho=0,\quad v\cdot n=0\text{ and }\curle v=-n^{\perp}\cdot B\cdot(v+c_{0}\nabla\rho^{-1})\text{ on }\partial\Omega\times(0,T);

(2)

if
(\rho,u)
satisfies (
B
), we impose

(B’)

\rho=\tilde{\rho},\quad v=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega\times(0,T)

and our strategy of the proof can be concluded as follows:

\mathrm{given}\,\,(\rho_{0},u_{0})\implies(\rho_{0},v_{0})\implies\exists\,(\rho,v)\implies\exists\,(\rho,u).

Unfortunately, for Theorem
1.5
, such decomposition may cause some serious problems when it comes to the boundary estimates, that is, if we extract
v
as we did above,
v|_{\partial\Omega}=-c_{0}\nabla\rho^{-1}
, which will hinder us to integrate by parts. As a consequence, we consider another type of decomposition
u=w+Q
coming from Lemma
2.4
. In every case that follows,
w
is divergence-free and enjoys vanished boundary condition and
Q
can be dominanted by
\nabla\rho
, which allows us to overcome the bounardy integrals, see Section
4
for details.

The scond part we are interested in is the local well-posedness for system (
1.1
). To prove Theorem
1.6
, we mainly follow the proof from Kim-Cho
[
11
]
by using the iterative appoarch. This method will be based on the linearized model associated with (
1.1
) , we refer to Section
5
for details.

To the proof of Theorem
1.7
, at least for the case when
(\rho,v)
satisfies condition (
A’
) or (
B’
), the key obeservation is that, if
\rho
is a weak solution of system (
1.1
) satisfying
\nabla\rho\in L^{s}(0,T;L^{r})
, then
(\rho,v)
is regular, since we can close the lower bounds for
(\rho,v)
merely under the condition (
1.13
). Here is an interpretation for (
1.13
) and (
1.14
): for
(\rho,u)
satisfying the condition (
A
) or (
B
), the
L^{s}(0,T;L^{r})
-norm of
v
does not blowup during the finite time
[0,T^{*})
, which is parallel to the classical Serrin’s condition for 2D non-homogeneous Navier-Stokes equations (
N
) (since, in such case, problem (
N
), at least for
\rho
away from the vacuum, automatically satisies the Serrin’s condition and admits a unique global strong solution without any smallness assumption, here
v
can be seen as the velocity field
u
in (
N
)). However, for the case when
(\rho,u)
satisfies the condition (
C
), we can not get rid off the the blowup behavior of
v
, since, in this case,
v|_{\partial\Omega}=-c_{0}\nabla\rho^{-1}
, which leads to some issue on the boundary estimates, we will come to this point again in Section
7
.

At last, we explain some techniques used in Section
3
and
7
. Since our main difficulty arises from the boundary integrals, in order to overcome it, we adapt the ideas from Cai-Li
[
7
]
: observing that the condition
v\cdot n|_{\partial\Omega}=0
leads to

v=(v\cdot n^{\perp})n^{\perp},

which implies that

\int_{\partial}v\cdot\nabla f=\int_{\partial}(v\cdot n^{\perp})n^{\perp}\cdot\nabla f=\int\nabla f\cdot\nabla^{\perp}(v\cdot n^{\perp}).

This observation can allow us to avoid some higher derivatives of
f
, which has advantages over directly using the trace inequality, since the latter needs the second order derivative of
f
.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we give some elementary results which will be used in later. Section
3
is devoted to the lower order estimates, compactness results for weak solutions and the higher order estimates for Theorem
1.3
–
1.4
, while Section
4
is devoted to the a priori estimets for Theorem
1.5
. In Section
5
, we will use the contraction mapping theorem to prove Theorem
1.6
and, then, in Section
6
, use this result to establish the global existence for Theorem
1.3
–
1.5
. At last, in Section
7
, we will give the proof of Theorem
1.7
.

2
Preliminaries

First, we give the following Gagliardo-Nirenberg’s inequalities which will be frequently used throughout the whole paper.

Lemma 2.1

(Gagliardo-Nirenberg’s inequality
[
26
,
30
]
)
.

For all
u_{i}\in H^{1}
,
i=1,2
,
q_{1}\in(2,\infty)
and
q_{2}\in(4,\infty)
, there exist positive constants
C_{i},\tilde{C}_{i}
depending on
q_{i},\Omega
,
i=1,2
, such that

\displaystyle\left\|u_{1}\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\left\|u_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2/{q_{1}}}\left\|\nabla u_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{1-2/{q_{1}}}+\tilde{C}_{1}\left\|u_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle\left\|u_{2}\right\|_{L^{q_{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\left\|u_{2}\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4/{q_{2}}}\left\|\nabla u_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{1-4/{q_{2}}}+\tilde{C}_{2}\left\|u_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

In particular, if
u_{i}
satisifies
u_{i}\cdot n=0
on
\partial\Omega
or
(u_{i})_{\Omega}=0
, then one can take
\tilde{C}_{1}=\tilde{C}_{2}=0
.

Lemma 2.2

(
[
3
,
38
]
)
.

Let
\Omega
be a simply connected bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with smooth boundary. Assume that
1<p<\infty
. There exists a positive constant
C=C(p,\Omega)
such that

\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\left(\left\|\dive{u}\right\|_{L^{p}}+\left\|\curle{u}\right\|_{L^{p}}\right),

for all
u\in W^{1,p}
with
u\cdot n=0
on
\partial\Omega
. Furthermore, for
u\in W^{2,p}
with
u\cdot n=0
on
\partial\Omega
, there exists a constant
C=C(p,\Omega)
such that

\left\|u\right\|_{W^{2,p}}\leq C\left(\left\|\dive u\right\|_{W^{1,p}}+\left\|\curle u\right\|_{W^{1,p}}+\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}}\right).

Remark 2.3
.

For case of use, we list the following equivalent norms for
\rho
satisfying the Neumann or the non-homogeneous Dirichlet condition. Let
1<p<\infty
, using Lemma
2.1
–
2.2
, if
\rho
satsifies the Neumann condition, one has, for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\rho_{\Omega}=(\rho_{0})_{\Omega},

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\|\nabla^{2}\rho\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{p}},

and

\displaystyle C^{-1}(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{p}}+(\rho_{0})_{\Omega})\leq\left\|\rho\right\|_{W^{1,p}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{p}}+(\rho_{0})_{\Omega}),

\displaystyle C^{-1}(\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{p}}+(\rho_{0})_{\Omega})\leq\left\|\rho\right\|_{W^{2,p}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{p}}+(\rho_{0})_{\Omega}),

\displaystyle C^{-1}(\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{p}}+(\rho_{0})_{\Omega})\leq\left\|\rho\right\|_{W^{3,p}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{p}}+(\rho_{0})_{\Omega}),

for some positive constant
C=C(p,\Omega)
.

If
\rho
satisfies the the non-homogeneous Dirichlet condition, then there exists a positive constant
C=C(p,\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle C^{-1}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{p}}\leq\left\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\right\|_{W^{1,p}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{p}},

\displaystyle C^{-1}\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{p}}\leq\left\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\right\|_{W^{2,p}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{p}},

\displaystyle C^{-1}\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{p}}\leq\left\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\right\|_{W^{3,p}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{p}}.

In both cases, the following Gagliardo-Nirenberg’s inequalities are established

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2/{q_{1}}}\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{1-2/{q_{1}}},

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{q_{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4/{q_{2}}}\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{1-4/{q_{2}}},

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{q_{1}}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2/{q_{1}}}\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{1-2/{q_{1}}}.

where
q_{1},\,q_{2}
as in Lemma
2.1
.

Next, for the problem

(2.1)

\begin{cases}\dive v=f,&x\in\Omega,\\
v=\Phi,&x\in\partial\Omega\end{cases}

one has the following conclusion which will be frequently used to eliminate the non-homogeneity of equations in Section
4
.

Lemma 2.4

(
[
16
]
, Theorem III.3.3)
.

Suppose that
\Phi\cdot n=0
on
\partial\Omega
and
f_{\Omega}=0
. Then,

1)

If
\Phi=0
, there exists a bounded linear operator
\mathcal{B}=\left[\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}\right]
,

\mathcal{B}:\{f\in L^{p}:f_{\Omega}=0\}\mapsto\left[W_{0}^{1,p}\right]^{2}

such that

\|\mathcal{B}[f]\|_{W^{1,p}}\leq C(p)\|f\|_{L^{p}},

for all
p\in(1,\infty)
, and the function
Q=\mathcal{B}[f]
solves the problem (
2.1
). Moreover, if
f=\dive g
with a certain
g\in L^{r},\left.g\cdot n\right|_{\partial\Omega}=0
, then for any
r\in(1,\infty)

\|\mathcal{B}[f]\|_{L^{r}}\leq C(r)\|g\|_{L^{r}}.

\mathcal{B}
is so-called the Bogovskiǐ operator.

2)

If
f=0
, there exists a bounded linear operator
\mathcal{C}=[\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}]
,

\mathcal{C}:\{\Phi:\Phi\cdot n|_{\partial\Omega}=0,\,\,\dive\Phi\in L^{p}\}\mapsto\left[W^{1,p}\right]^{2}

such that

\left\|\mathcal{C}[\Phi]\right\|_{W^{1,p}}\leq C(p)\left\|\dive\Phi\right\|_{L^{p}},

for all
p\in(1,\infty)
and the function
R=\mathcal{C}[\Phi]
sovles the problem (
2.1
).

Proof.

We only give a brief proof for
(2)
. By a simply change

\tilde{v}=v-\Phi,

it follows from (
2.1
) that
\tilde{v}
satisfies

(2.2)

\begin{cases}\dive\tilde{v}=-\dive\Phi,&x\in\Omega,\\
\tilde{v}=0,&x\in\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Thus, applying
(1)
for (
2.2
), we finish the proof.
∎

Lemma
2.5
–
2.9
are a series of results relating to the Stokes system which are vital to the higher order estimates of
v
and the construction of smooth initial data. These lemmas will be frequently used in Section
3
–
7
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\Omega
be a simply connected bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with smooth boundary. Let
(u,p)
satisfy the following equations

(2.3)

\begin{cases}-\Delta u+\nabla p=F,&x\in\Omega,\\
\dive u=0,&x\in\Omega,\end{cases}

where
F\in L^{2}
,
\int p=0
. There exists a positive constant
C
depending only on
\Omega
such that

(1)

if
u|_{\partial\Omega}=\Phi
, where
\Phi\in H^{2}
is a function defined on
\Omega
, then

(2.4)

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|p\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C(\|F\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\Phi\right\|_{H^{2}});

(2)

if
u\cdot n=0,\,\curle u=\varphi
on
\partial\Omega
,
where
\varphi\in H^{1}
is a function defined on
\Omega
, then

(2.5)

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|p\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C(\|F\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{1}}).

Proof.

Since (
2.4
) can be finded from
[
16
]
, Theorem IV.6.1, we only prove the case of slip condition. Using the identity
\Delta u=\nabla\dive u+\nabla^{\perp}\curle u
and integrating by parts, one has

\int|\curle u|^{2}-\int_{\partial}\varphi(u\cdot n^{\perp})=\int F\cdot u,

which implies that, using Lemma
2.1
–
2.2
and the trace inequality,

(2.6)

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C(\|F\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{1}}).

Since
\nabla p
is bounded in
H^{-1}
, it follows form the condition
\int p=0
that
p
is bounded in
L^{2}
. Next, taking
\curle
on the both side of
\eqref{equation2.1}_{1}
leads to

-\Delta(\curle u-\varphi)=\curle F-\Delta\varphi,

with boundary condition
\curle u-\varphi=0
. Then, using the regularity result of elliptic partial differential equations, we have

\left\|\curle u\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\|\curle F-\Delta\varphi\|_{H^{-1}}+C\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C(\|F\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{1}}).

Then, using again Lemma
2.2
and (
2.6
) gives

(2.7)

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{2}}\leq C(\|F\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}}).

and, consequently, the estimate of
p
is followed easily. It remains to omit the terms
\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}}
on the right-hand side of (
2.7
). Indeed, this is a simple consequence of the uniqueness of (
2.3
) and we leave the proof to the reader.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

Slimilar results for the Laplace equations
-\Delta u=F
instead of (
2.3
) with the same boundary conditions can be found in
[
17
]
.

Next, we give a lemma which indicates that
\rho\in C^{\gamma,\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\overline{Q}_{T})
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
provided
v
satisfying the Serrin’s condition. This result is critial to the estimate of
\Delta v
which will be used in Section
3
and
7
. The observation is based on the fact that
\dive v=0
.

Lemma 2.7

(
[
10
,
36
]
)
.

Let
v\in L^{s}(0,T;L^{r})
for some
r,s
satisfying (
1.15
),
\dive v=0
,
v\cdot n=0
and
\rho\in C([0,T];L^{2})\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})
be the weak solution of equation
\eqref{equation3.2}_{1}
(in the sense of distributions),
\alpha\leq\rho\leq\beta
. Let
\rho
satisfy either the Neumann condition

n\cdot\nabla\rho=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega\times(0,T)

or the non-homogeneous Dirichlet condition

\rho=\tilde{\rho}\text{ on }\partial\Omega\times(0,T).

Suppose that
\rho_{0}\in C^{\gamma_{0}}(\overline{\Omega})
for some
\gamma_{0}\in(0,1)
, then
\rho
is H
\ddot{o}
lder continuous. More precisely,
\rho\in C^{\gamma,\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\overline{Q}_{T})
, for some
\gamma
depending only on
\gamma_{0}
,
\alpha
and
\beta
.

Proof.

We only give the proof for
\rho|_{\partial\Omega}=\tilde{\rho}
, since the case for
\rho
satisfying the Neumann boundary condition has been proved in
[
10
,
36
]
. Let
\zeta
be a cut-off function,
\support\zeta\subset B_{r}\times[t_{0},t_{0}+\tau]
, where
B_{r}
is an arbitrary ball contained in
\Omega
and
[t_{0},t_{0}+\tau]\subset(0,T)
,
0<\tau<1
. Multiplying
\zeta^{2}(\rho-k)_{+}
on
\eqref{equation3.2}_{1}
and integrating by parts leads to

(2.8)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\tau]}\left\|\zeta(\rho-k)_{+}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\nu\left\|\zeta\nabla(\rho-k)_{+}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\zeta(\rho-k)_{+}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(t_{0})+C\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+\tau}\int_{\Omega}\left(\left|\nabla\zeta\right|^{2}+\zeta\left|\zeta_{t}\right|\right)(\rho-k)_{+}^{2}\,dxdt

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+\tau}\int_{\Omega}(v\cdot\nabla\rho)\zeta^{2}(\rho-k)_{+}\,dxdt.

For the last term on the right-hand side of (
2.8
), using Lemma
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+\tau}\int_{\Omega}(v\cdot\nabla\rho)\zeta^{2}(\rho-k)_{+}\,dxdt\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+\tau}\int_{\Omega}(v\cdot\nabla\zeta)\zeta(\rho-k)_{+}^{2}\,dxdt\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|v\right\|_{L^{\frac{2r}{r-2}}_{t}L^{r}_{x}}\left\|\zeta(\rho-k)_{+}\right\|_{L^{r}_{t}L^{\frac{2r}{r-2}}_{x}}\left\|\left|\nabla\zeta\right|(\rho-k)_{+}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}\tau^{\frac{rs-2s-2r}{2rs}}\left\|\left|\nabla\zeta\right|(\rho-k)_{+}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left(\sup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\tau]}\left\|\zeta(\rho-k)_{+}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\zeta\nabla(\rho-k)_{+}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}^{2}\right),

which, alonging with (
2.8
), implies that

(2.9)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\tau]}\left\|\zeta(\rho-k)_{+}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\nu\left\|\zeta\nabla(\rho-k)_{+}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\zeta(\rho-k)_{+}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(t_{0})+C\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+\tau}\int_{\Omega}\left(\left|\nabla\zeta\right|^{2}+\zeta\left|\zeta_{t}\right|\right)(\rho-k)_{+}^{2}\,dxdt.

The inequality above is valid for all
k\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, It follows from
[
25
]
Theorem 10.1 that
\rho\in C^{\gamma,\frac{\gamma}{2}}(Q_{T})
, for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

For the boundary estimates, if
\rho=\tilde{\rho}
on
\partial\Omega
, we still use
\zeta
and choose arbitrary
B_{r}\times[t_{0},t_{0}+\tau]\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]
, where
B_{r}
may intersect
\Omega
. Then, (
2.9
) holds for
k
sufficiently large, since
(\rho-k)_{+}
has vanished boundary, which implies that
\rho\in C^{\gamma,\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\overline{Q}_{T})
.
∎

Once
\rho
is H
\mathrm{\ddot{o}}
lder continuous,
\mu(\rho(x,t))
is continuous on
\overline{Q}_{T}
and, thus, we have the following estiamtes for the non-divergence type Stokes system.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
(v,p)
be a strong solution of the following Stokes system,

(2.10)

\begin{cases}-\mu(x)\Delta v+\nabla p=F,&x\in\Omega\\
\dive v=0,&x\in\Omega\end{cases}

where
\mu(x)\in C(\overline{\Omega})
,
\mu\in[\underline{\mu},\overline{\mu}]
,
\int p=0
and
F\in L^{2}
. Then there exists a positive constant
C
depending only on
\underline{\mu},\overline{\mu}
, continuity module of
\mu
and
\Omega
such that

(1)

if
u|_{\partial\Omega}=\Phi
, where
\Phi\in H^{2}
is a function defined on
\Omega
, then

(2.11)

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|p\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C(\|F\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\Phi\right\|_{H^{2}});

(2)

if
u\cdot n=0,\,\curle u=\varphi
on
\partial\Omega
,
where
\varphi\in H^{1}
is a function defined on
\Omega
, then

(2.12)

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|p\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C(\|F\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{1}}).

Proof.

The proof of Lemma
2.8
can be simply derived by using the freezing point argument, since we already have the conclusion when
\mu\equiv\mathrm{constant}
from Lemma
2.5
.
∎

Furthermore, in order to prove Lemma
4.3
(see Section
4
), we need the following auxiliary lemma. The purpose for using such result will be explained in the proof of Lemma
4.3
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
(v,p)
be a strong solution of the following Stokes system,

(2.13)

\begin{cases}-\dive[2\mu D(v)]+\nabla p=F,&x\in\Omega\\
\dive v=0,&x\in\Omega\end{cases}

where
\nabla\mu(\rho)\in L^{4}
,
\mu
is smooth and
0<\underline{\mu}\leq\mu\leq\overline{\mu}<\infty
,
\int p=0
and
F\in L^{2}
. Then there exists a positive constant
C
depending only on
\underline{\mu},\,\overline{\mu}
and
\Omega
such that

(1)

if
v|_{\partial\Omega}=\Phi
, where
\Phi\in H^{2}
is a function defined on
\Omega
, then

(2.14)

\left\|v\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|p\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\left[\left(\left\|\nabla\mu\right\|^{2}_{L^{4}}+1\right)\left(\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\Phi\right\|_{H^{1}}\right)+\left\|\nabla\mu\right\|^{2}_{L^{4}}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}\right];

(2)

if
v\cdot n=0,\,\curle v=\varphi
on
\partial\Omega
,
where
\varphi\in H^{1}
is a function defined on
\Omega
, then

(2.15)

\left\|v\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|p\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\left[\left(\left\|\nabla\mu\right\|^{2}_{L^{4}}+1\right)\left(\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{1}}\right)+\left\|\nabla\mu\right\|^{2}_{L^{4}}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}\right].

Proof.

We only give the proof for
(1)
. First of all, we can use Lemma
2.4
to find a function
R=\mathcal{C}[\Phi]
such that
\dive R=0
and
R|_{\partial\Omega}=\Phi
, then
\eqref{8.13}_{1}
becomes

-\dive[2\mu(\rho)D(v-R)]+\nabla p=F+\dive[2\mu(\rho)D(R)].

Using standard energy approach and the fact
\left\|R\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\left\|\nabla\Phi\right\|_{L^{2}}
, one has

(2.16)

\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|p\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(\left\|F\right\|_{H^{-1}}+\left\|\nabla\Phi\right\|_{L^{2}})\leq C(\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\Phi\right\|_{H^{1}}).

Next, rewritting
\eqref{8.13}_{1}
into the form

-\Delta v+\nabla\left[\frac{p}{\mu(\rho)}\right]=\frac{F}{\mu(\rho)}+\frac{2\mu^{\prime}\nabla\rho\cdot D(v)}{\mu(\rho)}-\frac{p\mu^{\prime}}{\mu(\rho)^{2}}\nabla\rho,

using Lemma
2.5
, we have

\left\|v\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|p\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\left[\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\Phi\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\nabla\mu(\rho)\right\|_{L^{4}}\left(\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{4}}+\left\|p\right\|_{L^{4}}\right)\right],

which, using Lemma
2.1
and (
2.16
), leads to

\left\|v\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|p\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\left[\left(\left\|\nabla\mu(\rho)\right\|^{2}_{L^{4}}+1\right)\left(\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\Phi\right\|_{H^{1}}\right)+\left\|\nabla\mu(\rho)\right\|^{2}_{L^{4}}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}\right].

Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

At last, in subsection
3.2
and Section
6
, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 2.10

(Simon
[
31
,
34
]
)
.

Let
X\hookrightarrow B\hookrightarrow Y
be three Banach spaces with compact imbedding
X\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow Y
. Further, let there eixst
0<\theta<1
and
M>0
such that

\left\|v\right\|_{B}\leq M\left\|v\right\|_{X}^{1-\theta}\left\|v\right\|_{Y}^{\theta},\,\,\,\text{for all}\,\,v\in X\cap Y.

Denote for
T>0
,

W(0,T):=W^{s_{0},r_{0}}(0,T;X)\cap W^{s_{1},r_{1}}(0,T;Y)

with
s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
r_{1},r_{0}\in[1,\infty]
, and

s_{\theta}:=(1-\theta)s_{0}+\theta s_{1},\,\,\frac{1}{r_{\theta}}:=\frac{1-\theta}{r_{0}}+\frac{\theta}{r_{1}},\,\,s^{*}:=s_{\theta}-\frac{1}{r_{\theta}}.

Assume that
s_{\theta}>0
and
F
is a bounded set in
W(0,T)
.

(1)

If
s^{*}\leq 0
, then
F
is precompact in
L^{p}(0,T;B)
for all
1\leq p<-\frac{1}{s^{*}}
.

(2)

If
s^{*}>0
, then
F
is precompact in
C([0,T];B)
.

3
A Priori Estimates (I): Case (
A
) and (
B
)

In this section, we are going to establish the a priori bounds for
(\rho,v)
which will be used in the proof of Theorem
1.3
. Throughout this section, let
T\in(0,\infty)
and
(\rho,v)
be a smooth solution to (
1.18
) with smooth data
(\rho_{0},v_{0})
. Moreover, in order to simplify the notation, we always denote by
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, the arbitrarily small number belongs to
(0,1/2]
, and we use the subscripts
C_{\varepsilon}
,
C_{\varepsilon_{i}}
to emphasize the dependency of the constant
C
on
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{i}

3.1
Lower Order Estimates

The first lemma is a consequence of the standard maximal principle.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\alpha\leq\rho_{0}\leq\beta
and
(\rho,v)
satisfy the condition (
A’
) or (
B’
), one has

\alpha\leq\rho(x,t)\leq\beta
for
x\in\Omega
and all
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

We only prove the upper bound, since the lower one can be derived in a similar way. Using (
1.17
), we convert the equation (
1.18
) into the form

(3.1)

\rho_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\rho-c_{0}\Delta\log\rho=0.

If
(\rho,v)
satisfies the condition (
A’
), set
k=\beta
and multiply (
3.1
) by
(\rho-k)_{+}:=\max\{\rho-k,0\}
. After integrating by parts, we obtain

(3.2)

\frac{d}{dt}\int\frac{1}{2}(\rho-k)_{+}^{2}+\int c_{0}\rho^{-1}\left|\nabla(\rho-k)_{+}\right|^{2}=0,

where we have used the identity

\int v\cdot\nabla\rho(\rho-k)_{+}=\int v\cdot\nabla(\rho-k)_{+}(\rho-k)_{+}=0,

since
v
is divergence-free and
v\cdot n=0
on
\partial\Omega
. Hence, integrating (
3.2
) from
0
to
T
and then, using (
1.7
) implies that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int(\rho-k)_{+}^{2}\leq\int(\rho_{0}-k)_{+}^{2}=0,

which implies that
\rho\leq k=\beta
for all
x\in\Omega
and
t\in[0,T]
. The case when
(\rho,v)
satisfies the condition (
B’
) can be proved analogously. Thus, we complete the proof of Lemma
3.1
.
∎

Our main purpose in this subsection is establishing the lower order bounds. We aim to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
(\rho,v)
satisfy the condition (
A’
) or (
B’
). Suppose that

(3.3)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|^{4}_{L^{4}}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)\,dt\leq 2.

There exists a positive constant
\delta
depending on
\Omega
,
c_{0}
,
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\left\|v_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}
such that, if
\left\|\nabla\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq\delta
,

(3.4)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|^{4}_{L^{4}}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)\,dt\leq 1.

We give the proof of Proposition
3.2
in several steps. First, we estimate the first order derivative of
\rho
, which is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

There exists a positive constant
C
depending only on
c_{0}
and
\beta
such that, if
(\rho,v)
satisfies the condition (
A’
),

(3.5)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}\leq C\left\|\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}};

if
(\rho,v)
satisfies the condition (
B’
),

(3.6)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}\leq C\left\|\rho_{0}-\tilde{\rho}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

Multiplying (
3.1
) by
\rho
(if
\rho
satisfies the condition (
B’
), multiply
\rho-\tilde{\rho}
), integrating over
\Omega
and computing in the same way of Lemma
3.1
, one has

(3.7)

\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+2c_{0}\beta^{-1}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq 0.

Using Gr
\mathrm{\ddot{o}}
nwall’s inequality leads to

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}+\sqrt{2c_{0}\beta^{-1}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}\leq\left\|\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}},

where we use the fact that
\rho\leq\beta
from Lemma
3.1
. This completes the proof.
∎

Next, the following lemma shows that the second order derivative of
\rho
can be dominated by the norm of
v
provided
\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}(t)
is small enough.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
(\rho,v)
satisfy the condition (
A’
) or (
B’
). Then there exist a positive constant
\delta_{1}
depending on
\Omega
,
c_{0}
,
\alpha
,
\beta
and a positive constant
C
depending on
\Omega
,
c_{0}
,
\alpha
and
\beta
such that, if
\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}(t)\leq\delta_{1}
for all
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.8)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\,dt\leq\exp\left\{C\int_{0}^{T}\left\|v\right\|^{4}_{L^{4}}\,dt\right\}\left\|\nabla\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Proof.

Multiplying (
1.18
) by
(-\Delta\rho)
and integrating over
\Omega
, we obtain

\frac{d}{dt}\int\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla\rho\right|^{2}+\int c_{0}\rho^{-1}\left|\Delta\rho\right|^{2}=\int(v\cdot\nabla\rho)\Delta\rho-\int c_{0}\rho^{-2}\left|\nabla\rho\right|^{2}\Delta\rho,

which implies that, using Lemma
3.1
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla\rho\right|^{2}+c_{0}\beta^{-1}\int\left|\Delta\rho\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\int\left(\left|\nabla\rho\right|^{2}+\left|v\right|\left|\nabla\rho\right|\right)\left|\Delta\rho\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\int\left(\left|\nabla\rho\right|^{4}+\left|v\right|^{2}\left|\nabla\rho\right|^{2}\right)+c_{0}(2\beta)^{-1}\int\left|\Delta\rho\right|^{2}.

Hence, by Lemma
2.1
and
3.1
, we have

(3.9)

\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\nu\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\left\|v\right\|^{4}_{L^{4}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

for some positive constant
\nu=\nu(c_{0},\beta)
and
C=C(\Omega,c_{0},\alpha,\beta)
. Thus, if we choose
\delta_{1}=\nu^{1/2}(2C)^{-1/2}
and set
\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}(t)\leq\delta_{1}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, using the Gr
\mathrm{\ddot{o}}
nwall’s inequality, we can deduce from (
3.9
) and Lemma
2.1
that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\nu\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\,dt\leq\exp\left\{C\int_{0}^{T}\left\|v\right\|^{4}_{L^{4}}\,dt\right\}\left\|\nabla\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

which concludes the proof of (
3.8
). The case when
(\rho,v)
satisfies the condition (
B’
) can be computed in the same way, since
\rho_{t}
has vanished boundary.
∎

From the observation of Lemma
3.4
, in order to derive the bounds for
\rho
, we need to control the
L^{4}(0,T;L^{4})
norm of
v
, which is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
(\rho,v)
satisfy the condition (
A’
) or (
B’
). Suppose that condition (
3.3
) holds.
Then there exists a positive constant
C
depending on
\Omega
,
c_{0}
,
\alpha
and
\beta
such that

(3.10)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|v\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left\|v\right\|^{4}_{L^{4}}+\left\|\nabla v\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)\,dt\leq C(1+\left\|v_{0}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}).

Proof.

In order to simplify our proof, we only consider the case when
\rho
satisfies (
B’
) and
v
satisfies (
A’
), since other cases can be established in the same way and are much easier. We first deal with a special case for
\curle v=-n^{\perp}\cdot B\cdot v
on the boundary.

We write
\eqref{equation3.2}_{2}
, using (
1.17
), into the form

(3.11)

\displaystyle\rho v_{t}+\rho u\cdot\nabla v-\dive{[2\mu D(v)]}+\nabla\pi_{1}

\displaystyle=c_{0}\dive{\left(2\mu\nabla^{2}\rho^{-1}\right)}-c_{0}\dive{\left(\rho v\otimes\nabla\rho^{-1}\right)}-c_{0}^{2}\dive{\left(\rho\nabla\rho^{-1}\otimes\nabla\rho^{-1}\right)},

Multiplying (
3.11
) by
v
and integrating over
\Omega
, one has

(3.12)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int\frac{1}{2}\rho\left|v\right|^{2}-\int\dive{[2\mu D(v)]}\cdot v

\displaystyle=\int c_{0}\dive{\left(2\mu\nabla^{2}\rho^{-1}\right)}\cdot v-\int c_{0}\dive{\left(\rho v\otimes\nabla\rho^{-1}\right)}\cdot v

\displaystyle-\int c_{0}^{2}\dive{\left(\rho\nabla\rho^{-1}\otimes\nabla\rho^{-1}\right)}\cdot v:=\sum_{i=1}^{3}I_{i}.

Next, for the last term on the left-hand side of (
3.12
), we use again Lemma
2.1
and
3.1
to get

(3.13)

\displaystyle-\int\dive{[2\mu D(v)]}\cdot v

\displaystyle=-\int 2\mu\Delta v\cdot v-\int 2\mu^{\prime}\nabla\rho\cdot D(v)\cdot v

\displaystyle=\int 2\mu|\curle v|^{2}+\int_{\partial}2\mu v\cdot B\cdot v

\displaystyle+\int 2\mu^{\prime}\nabla^{\perp}\rho\cdot v(\curle v)-\int 2\mu^{\prime}\nabla\rho\cdot D(v)\cdot v,

\displaystyle\geq\underline{\mu}\int\left|\curle v\right|^{2}-\left(C_{\varepsilon}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\sqrt{\rho}v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right),

where
\underline{\mu}:=\min_{s\in[\alpha,\beta]}\mu(s)
and the last inequality follows from the fact that
B
is positive semi-definite.

For
I_{1}
, we use the similar approach and write it in the component form,

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial}-2c_{0}\mu\partial_{ij}\rho^{-1}v_{i}n_{j}+\int 2c_{0}\mu\partial_{ij}\rho^{-1}\partial_{j}v_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial}-4c_{0}\mu\rho^{-3}\partial_{i}\rho\partial_{j}\rho v_{i}n_{j}+\int_{\partial}2c_{0}\mu\rho^{-2}\partial_{ij}\rho v_{i}n_{j}+\int 2c_{0}\mu\partial_{ij}\rho^{-1}\partial_{j}v_{i}:=\sum_{i=1}^{3}J_{i}.

The estimate of
J_{3}
can be simply derived by using Lemma
2.1
and
3.1
, that is,

(3.14)

\displaystyle|J_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq C\left|\int\mu\left(2\rho^{-3}\partial_{i}\rho\partial_{j}\rho-\rho^{-2}\partial_{ij}\rho\right)\partial_{j}v_{i}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2})+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

To the boundary parts
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
, it suffices to estimate

\displaystyle J_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial}\phi(\rho)\partial_{i}\rho\partial_{j}\rho v_{i}n_{j},

\displaystyle J_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial}\phi(\rho)\partial_{ij}\rho v_{i}n_{j}=-\int_{\partial}\phi(\rho)v_{i}\partial_{i}n_{j}\partial_{j}\rho,

where
\phi(\cdot)
is a positive smooth function defined on
(0,\infty)
. Using Lemma
2.1
and
3.1
, one has

(3.15)

\displaystyle|J_{1}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\partial}\phi(\rho)(n\cdot\nabla\rho)(v\cdot n^{\perp})n^{\perp}\cdot\nabla\rho\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int\nabla^{\perp}[\phi(\rho)(n\cdot\nabla\rho)]\cdot\nabla\rho(v\cdot n^{\perp})+\int\phi(\rho)(n\cdot\nabla\rho)\nabla\rho\cdot\nabla^{\perp}(v\cdot n^{\perp})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+C_{\varepsilon_{1}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\sqrt{\rho}v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

and

(3.16)

\displaystyle|J_{2}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\partial}\phi(\rho)(v\cdot n^{\perp})n^{\perp}\cdot\nabla n\cdot\nabla\rho\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int\nabla^{\perp}\phi(\rho)\cdot(\nabla n\cdot\nabla\rho)(v\cdot n^{\perp})-\int_{\Omega}\phi(\rho)\nabla^{\perp}\cdot(\nabla n\cdot\nabla\rho)(v\cdot n^{\perp})\,dx\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\int\phi(\rho)\nabla^{\perp}(v\cdot n^{\perp})\cdot(\nabla n\cdot\nabla\rho)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+C_{\varepsilon_{2}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\sqrt{\rho}v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{2}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Combining (
3.14
)–(
3.16
), we deduce that

(3.17)

|I_{1}|\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+C_{\varepsilon}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\sqrt{\rho}v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Similar computation can be applied for
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
, that is,

(3.18)

\displaystyle|I_{2}|\leq C_{\varepsilon_{3}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\sqrt{\rho}v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{3}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

(3.19)

\displaystyle|I_{3}|\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+C_{\varepsilon_{4}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\sqrt{\rho}v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{4}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Therefore, we go back to the estimate of
v
, combining (
3.12
)–(
3.13
) and (
3.17
)–(
3.19
) and then, using Lemma
2.2
implies that

(3.20)

\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\sqrt{\rho}v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\nu\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\left(1+\left\|\sqrt{\rho}v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right),

for some constant
C
depending on
\Omega
,
c_{0}
,
\alpha
and
\beta
. In view of the condition (
3.3
), we obtain the bound (
3.10
) via Gr
\mathrm{\ddot{o}}
nwall’s inequality and Lemma
2.1
. For the general case when
\curle v=-n^{\perp}\cdot B\cdot(v+c_{0}\nabla\rho^{-1})
on
\partial\Omega\times(0,T)
, we can also obtain the desire bounds (
3.10
) by calculating the extra boundary term

\int_{\partial}2c_{0}\mu v\cdot B\cdot\nabla\rho^{-1}.

However, this term is nothing but a special case of
J^{\prime}_{2}
with
\nabla n
replaced by
B
. Therefore, following the same computation of
J^{\prime}_{2}
, we complete the proof for the general case.
∎

Now, we can turn back to prove Proposition
3.2
.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Using Lemma
3.5
, we obtain the bound of
v
, (
3.10
), under the condition (
3.3
).
Next, using Lemma
3.4
and (
3.10
) leads to (
3.8
), that is, if
\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}(t)\leq\delta_{1}
for all
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.21)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\,dt\leq C\left\|\nabla\rho_{0}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

where
C
is a positive constant depending on
\Omega
,
c_{0}
,
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\left\|v_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}
.

Hence, if we set the constant
\delta>0
such that

(3.22)

\delta=\min\left\{C^{-\frac{1}{2}},\delta_{1}C^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right\},

and let
\left\|\nabla\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq\delta
, then it is easy to check that (
3.4
) is established. Consequently, we complete the proof.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

It follows from the equation
\eqref{equation3.2}_{1}
and
(\nabla\rho,v)\in L^{4}(0,T;L^{4})
that
\rho_{t}
is bounded in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})
.

3.2
Compactness Results

Before establishing higher order estimates, we tend to prove the compactness results for
(\rho,v)
, which plays a crucial role in the proof of Theorem
1.3
, see Section
6
. Concerning a sequence of weak solutions
(\rho^{n},v^{n})
with
\pi_{1}
replaced by
\pi_{1}^{n}
satisfying the condition (
A’
) or (
B’
) and the initial conditions

(3.23)

\rho^{n}|_{t=0}=\rho^{n}_{0},\,\,v^{n}|_{t=0}=v^{n}_{0}.

We assume that
(\rho^{n},v^{n})
satisfy, uniformly in
n\geq 1
, the a priori bounds that derived in the preceeding section and
\nabla\rho_{0}^{n},v_{0}^{n}\xrightarrow{\,\,s\,\,}\nabla\rho_{0},v_{0}
in
L^{2}
. Without loss of generality, extracting subsequences if necessary, we assume

(3.24)

\begin{cases}\rho^{n}\xrightharpoonup{w^{*}}\rho\,\,&\mathrm{in}\,\,L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1}),\\
\rho^{n}\xrightharpoonup{\,\,w\,}\rho\,\,&\mathrm{in}\,\,L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}),\\
v^{n}\xrightharpoonup{w^{*}}v\,\,&\mathrm{in}\,\,L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}),\\
v^{n}\xrightharpoonup{\,\,w\,}v\,\,&\mathrm{in}\,\,L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}).\end{cases}

We may now state our compactness results whose proof is followed by
[
27
]
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Under the hypothesis above, we have, for all
p\in[1,\infty)
,

(3.25)

\displaystyle\rho^{n}\xrightarrow{\,\,s\,\,}\rho\quad

\displaystyle\mathrm{in}\,\,C([0,T];L^{p}),

(3.26)

\displaystyle v^{n}\xrightarrow{\,\,s\,\,}v\quad

\displaystyle\mathrm{in}\,\,L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}).

Proof.

Since we have
\eqref{equat4.3}_{2}
and
\rho^{n}_{t}
is bounded in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})
from Remark
3.6
, (
3.25
) can be directly derived by using Lemma
2.10
. To prove (
3.26
), observing that
\eqref{equation3.2}_{2}
leads to

\left|\left<(\rho^{n}v^{n})_{t},\phi\right>\right|\leq C\left\|\phi\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})},

for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega\times[0,T])
such that
\dive\phi=0
, which implies that
(\rho^{n}v^{n})_{t}
is bounded in
L^{2}(0,T;V^{-1,2})
. On the other hand, since
\rho^{n}v^{n}
is bounded in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})
, it follows from Lemma
2.10
that
\rho^{n}v^{n}
is precompact in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})
, that is,

(3.27)

\rho^{n}v^{n}\xrightarrow{\,\,s\,\,}\overline{\rho v}\quad\text{in }L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}).

Thanks to
\eqref{equat4.3}_{4}
and (
3.25
), we have

\overline{\rho v}=\rho v,\quad v^{n}\xrightarrow{\,\,s\,\,}v\quad\text{in }L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}),

which gives (
3.26
).
∎

3.3
Higher Order Estimates

In this subsection, we will show the a priori estimates for strong solutions of (
1.18
). We still use the assumption at the begining of Section
3
. Furthermore, throughout this subsection, we always keep

\left\|\nabla\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq\delta

small enough so that Proposition
3.2
is valid. In a word, we have all the estimates of
(\rho,v)
from Lemma
3.1
–
3.5
.

For convenience, we set

\displaystyle F(t):=\left\|\nabla v\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle G(t):=\left\|\Delta v\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|v_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\rho_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}(t):=\int_{\partial}\mu v\cdot B\cdot v,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}(t):=\int c_{0}\mu\nabla^{\perp}(v\cdot n^{\perp})\cdot B\cdot\nabla\rho^{-1}.

We now state the proposition we are aimming for in this subsection.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
(\rho,v)
satisfy the condition (
A’
) or (
B’
). Then

(3.28)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}F(t)+\int_{0}^{T}\left(G(t)+\left\|\pi\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\right)\,dt\leq C,

where
C
is a positive constant depending on
\Omega
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
c_{0}
,
\left\|\rho_{0}\right\|_{H^{2}}
and
\left\|v_{0}\right\|_{H^{1}}
.

In order to prove Proposition
3.8
, we need several auxiliary lemmas.

Lemma 3.9
.

Under the assumptions at the begining of this section,

(1)

if
(\rho,v)
satisfies the condition (
A’
), then, for all
\varepsilon_{1}\in(0,1/2]
,

(3.29)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\nabla\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1}}\mathcal{A}_{1}(t)F(t)+\varepsilon_{1}\left(\left\|v\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}+\left\|v_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right);

(2)

if
(\rho,v)
satisfies the condition (
B’
), then

(3.30)

\left\|\Delta\log\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\left(\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla v\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)

and for all
\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}\in(0,1]
,

(3.31)

\frac{d}{dt}\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\varepsilon_{2}}\mathcal{A}_{2}(t)\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{2}\left\|v_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(3.32)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\Delta\log\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon_{3}}\mathcal{A}_{3}(t)\left(\left\|\Delta\log\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+C_{\varepsilon_{3}}\left\|\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{3}\left\|\Delta v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Here,
C
,
C_{\varepsilon_{1}}-C_{\varepsilon_{3}}
are positive constants depending on
\Omega
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
c_{0}
with
C_{\varepsilon_{1}}-C_{\varepsilon_{3}}
extra depending on
\varepsilon_{1}
–
\varepsilon_{3}
respectively,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
–
\mathcal{A}_{3}
are all nonnegative integrable functions defined on
[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

We first consider the case when
(\rho,v)
satisfies the condition (
A’
). Taking
-(\nabla\Delta\rho)\nabla
on the both sides of
\eqref{equation3.2}_{1}
and integrating by parts, we have

(3.33)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int\frac{1}{2}\left|\Delta\rho\right|^{2}+\int c_{0}\rho^{-1}\left|\nabla\Delta\rho\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\nabla\Delta\rho\cdot\nabla v\cdot\nabla\rho+\int v\cdot\nabla^{2}\rho\cdot\nabla\Delta\rho

\displaystyle-\int 2c_{0}\rho^{-3}\left|\nabla\rho\right|^{2}\nabla\rho\cdot\nabla\Delta\rho+\int c_{0}\rho^{-2}\nabla(\left|\nabla\rho\right|^{2})\cdot\nabla\Delta\rho

\displaystyle+\int c_{0}\rho^{-2}\Delta\rho\nabla\rho\cdot\nabla\Delta\rho

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{5}K_{i}.

For
K_{1}
–
K_{5}
, we use Lemma
2.1
and
3.1
to find that

(3.34)

\begin{cases}\left|K_{1}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{2}\left\|\Delta v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
\left|K_{2}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon_{3}}\left\|v\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{3}\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
\left|K_{3}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon_{4}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{6}}^{6}+\varepsilon_{4}\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
\quad\quad\,\leq C_{\varepsilon_{4}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{4}\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
\left|K_{4}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon_{5}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{5}\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
\left|K_{5}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon_{6}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{6}\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.\end{cases}

Combining (
3.33
) and (
3.34
), we have, for some
\nu>0
,

(3.35)

\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\nu\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|v\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\right)\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C_{\varepsilon}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|\Delta v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

Next, we estimate the bound of
\rho_{t}
. Differentiating
\eqref{equation3.2}_{1}
with respect to
t
, one has

(3.36)

\rho_{tt}-c_{0}\rho^{-1}\Delta\rho_{t}=-v_{t}\cdot\nabla\rho-v\cdot\nabla\rho_{t}+2c_{0}\rho^{-3}\rho_{t}\left|\nabla\rho\right|^{2}-c_{0}\rho^{-1}\left(\left|\nabla\rho\right|^{2}\right)_{t}-c_{0}\rho^{-2}\rho_{t}\Delta\rho.

Multiplying
\rho_{t}
on both sides of (
3.36
) and integrating over
\Omega
, we have

(3.37)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int\frac{1}{2}\left|\rho_{t}\right|^{2}+\nu\int\left|\nabla\rho_{t}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int\left|v_{t}\right|\left|\nabla\rho\right|\left|\rho_{t}\right|+C\int\left|\rho_{t}\right|^{2}\left|\nabla\rho\right|^{2}

\displaystyle+\int\left|\rho_{t}\right|\left|\nabla\rho_{t}\right|\left|\nabla\rho\right|+C\int\left|\rho_{t}\right|^{2}\left|\Delta\rho\right|

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{4}L_{i}.

Similarly, we use Lemma
2.1
and
3.1
to obtain

(3.38)

\begin{cases}\left|L_{1}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\left\|v_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{2}\left\|\nabla\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\\
\left|L_{2}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon_{3}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{3}\left\|\nabla\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\\
\left|L_{3}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon_{4}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{4}\left\|\nabla\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\\
\left|L_{4}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon_{5}}\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{5}\left\|\nabla\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.\end{cases}

Thus, from (
3.37
) and (
3.38
), one has, for some
\nu>0
,

(3.39)

\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\nu\left\|\nabla\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|v_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

Combining (
3.35
) and (
3.39
) leads to the estimate (
3.29
).

Next, we trun to the case when
(\rho,v)
satisfies the condition (
B’
). The main difficulty in this case is that, although we still have the estimate (
3.39
), we can not use the energy method by integrating by parts to derive the bound of
\nabla\Delta\rho
. To overcome it, we estimate directly from
\eqref{equation3.2}_{1}
. More precisely, we first renormalize
\eqref{equation3.2}_{1}
by writting

(3.40)

(\log\rho)_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\log\rho-c_{0}\rho^{-1}\Delta\log\rho=0.

Next, differentiating in
x
on both sides of (
3.40
), one has

(3.41)

\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}+\nabla v\cdot\nabla\log\rho+v\cdot\nabla^{2}\log\rho+c_{0}\rho^{-2}\nabla\rho\Delta\log\rho-c_{0}\rho^{-1}\nabla\Delta\log\rho=0.

Then, applying
L^{2}
-norm for
\nabla\Delta\log\rho
, then, using Lemma
2.1
and
3.1
leads to

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\Delta\log\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\left|\nabla v\right|\!\cdot\!\left|\nabla\log\rho\right|\right\|_{L^{2}}+\|\left|v\right|\!\cdot\!|\nabla^{2}\log\rho|\|_{L^{2}}+\|\left|\nabla\rho\right|\!\cdot\!|\Delta\log\rho|\|_{L^{2}}\right).

Thus, using Lemma
2.1
, for all
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in(0,1/2]
, there exists a constant
C_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}
depending on
\Omega
,
c_{0}
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\Delta\log\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}\left(\left\|\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|v\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\Delta\log\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\Delta\log\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+C_{\varepsilon_{2}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\left\|\nabla\Delta\log\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{2}\left\|\Delta v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

Consequently,

(3.42)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\Delta\log\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}\left\|\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C_{\varepsilon}\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|v\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\right)\left\|\Delta\log\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+C_{\varepsilon}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|\Delta v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

which gives (
3.32
).

It remains to show (
3.30
) and (
3.31
). From (
3.40
), we use Lemma
2.1
and
3.1
to obtain,

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta\log\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+C\left\|\nabla\log\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{4}}\left\|v\right\|^{2}_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+C_{\varepsilon}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\left\|v\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\left\|\nabla v\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon\left\|\Delta\log\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

that is,

\left\|\Delta\log\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\left(\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla v\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right).

For (
3.31
), we can follow the proof from (
3.36
) to (
3.39
) by applying
(\log\rho)_{t}\partial_{t}
on both sided (
3.40
) and integrating over
\Omega
, that is,

(3.43)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int\frac{1}{2}|(\log\rho)_{t}|^{2}+\int c_{0}\rho^{-1}|\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\int c_{0}\rho^{-1}\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}\cdot\nabla\log\rho(\log\rho)_{t}-\int v_{t}\cdot\nabla\log\rho(\log\rho)_{t}

\displaystyle+\int c_{0}\rho^{-1}|(\log\rho)_{t}|^{2}|\nabla\log\rho|^{2}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{3}P_{i},

where, applying Lemma
2.1
and
3.1
,

(3.44)

\begin{cases}|P_{1}|&\!\!\!\!\leq C\left\|\nabla\log\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{4}}\left\|\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\!\!\!\!\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\left\|\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
|P_{2}|&\!\!\!\!\leq\left\|\nabla\log\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{4}}\left\|v_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\!\!\!\!\leq C_{\varepsilon_{2}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{2}\left\|v_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
|P_{3}|&\!\!\!\!\leq\left\|\nabla\log\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{4}}\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{4}}\\
&\!\!\!\!\leq C_{\varepsilon_{3}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{3}\left\|\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.\end{cases}

Combining (
3.43
) and (
3.44
) leads to

\frac{d}{dt}\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\nu\left\|\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|v_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 3.10
.

One may find that we estimate
\log\rho
instead of
\rho
in the proof of
(\rho,v)
satisfying (
B’
). This is based on the observation that
\eqref{equation3.2}_{1}
has the dissipative term
\Delta\log\rho
, that is,

\rho_{t}+v\cdot\nabla\rho-c_{0}\Delta\log\rho=0.

Thus, such conversion can avoid the occurrence of the nonlinear term
|\nabla\rho|^{2}
, otherwise, if we estimate
\Delta\rho
in the proof of (
3.30
), we need additional smallness assumption on
\nabla\rho_{0}
to handle
\left\||\nabla\rho|^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}
, which is not what we expect (this point will also be seen in Section
7
).

The next lemma shows that
v
can be bounded by the norm of
\rho
provided
\left\|\nabla\rho_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}
is small.

Lemma 3.11
.

Under the assumptions at the begining of this section,

(1)

if
(\rho,v)
satisfy the condition (
A’
), then for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2]
,

(3.45)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(\mathcal{M}_{1}(t)+\|\sqrt{\mu}\curle v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+\left\|v_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{M}_{2}(t)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{A}_{4}(t)F(t)+\varepsilon\left(\left\|\nabla\rho_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+\mathcal{A}_{5}(t),

and

(3.46)

\displaystyle\left\|v\right\|^{2}_{H^{2}}+\left\|\pi\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq\mathcal{A}_{6}(t)F(t)+C\left(\left\|\nabla\Delta\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|v_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+\mathcal{A}_{7}(t),

where
C
and
C_{\varepsilon}
are positive constants depending on
\Omega
,
c_{0}
,
\alpha
,
\beta
with
C_{\varepsilon}
extra depending on
\varepsilon
;
\mathcal{A}_{4}
–
\mathcal{A}_{7}
are nonnegative integrable functions defined on
[0,\infty)
;

(2)

if
(\rho,v)
satisfies the conditin (
B’
), one still has the estimates (
3.45
) and (
3.46
) with
\mathcal{M}_{1}(t)=\mathcal{M}_{2}(t)=0
in (
3.45
). More precisely,

(3.47)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\sqrt{\mu}|D(v)|\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|v_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{A}_{8}(t)\left(\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\Delta\log\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla\Delta\log\rho\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

and

(3.48)

\displaystyle\left\|v\right\|^{2}_{H^{2}}+\left\|\pi\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{A}_{9}(t)\left(\left\|\nabla v\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|\Delta\log\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla\Delta\log\rho\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+C\left(\left\|v_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla(\log\rho)_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right).

where
C
and
C_{\varepsilon}
as in
(1)
;
\mathcal{A}_{8}
and
\mathcal{A}_{9}
are nonnegative integrable functions defined on
[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

Rewrite
\eqref{equation3.2}_{2}
as

(3.49)

\displaystyle\rho v_{t}-\dive{[2\mu D(v)]}+\nabla\pi_{1}

\displaystyle=-\rho u\cdot\nabla v+c_{0}\dive{\left(2\mu\nabla^{2}\rho^{-1}\right)}-c_{0}\dive{\left(\rho v\otimes\nabla\rho^{-1}\right)}

\displaystyle-c_{0}^{2}\dive{\left(\rho\nabla\rho^{-1}\otimes\nabla\rho^{-1}\right)}.

We first come to the case when
(\rho,v)
satisfies the condition (
A’
) and consider the special case when
\curle v=-n^{\perp}\cdot B\cdot v
on
\partial\Omega\times(0,T)
. Multiplying
v_{t}
on both sides of (
3.49
) and integrating over
\Omega
, one gets

(3.50)

\displaystyle\int\rho\left|v_{t}\right|^{2}-\int\dive[2\mu D(v)]\cdot v_{t}

\displaystyle=-\int\rho u\cdot\nabla v\cdot v_{t}+\int c_{0}\dive{\left(2\mu\nabla^{2}\rho^{-1}\right)}\cdot v_{t}-\int c_{0}\dive{\left(\rho v\otimes\nabla\rho^{-1}\right)}\cdot v_{t}

\displaystyle-\int c_{0}^{2}\dive{\left(\rho\nabla\rho^{-1}\otimes\nabla\rho^{-1}\right)}\cdot v_{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{4}M_{i}.

For the second term on the left-hand side of (
3.50
), we have

\displaystyle-\int\dive[2\mu D(v)]\cdot v_{t}

\displaystyle=-\int 2\mu\Delta v\cdot v_{t}-\int 2\mu^{\prime}\nabla\rho\cdot D(v)\cdot v_{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{i}.

First, to estimate
Q_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle=-\int 2\mu\nabla^{\perp}(\curle v)\cdot v_{t}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\partial}2\mu\curle v(v_{t}\cdot n^{\perp})+\int\mu\frac{d}{dt}|\curle v|^{2}+\int\mu^{\prime}\nabla^{\perp}\rho\cdot v_{t}(\curle v)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left(\mathcal{M}_{1}(t)+\|\sqrt{\mu}\curle v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\partial}\mu_{t}(v\cdot B\cdot v)-\int\mu_{t}|\curle v|^{2}+\int\mu^{\prime}\nabla^{\perp}\rho\cdot v_{t}(\curle v),

where, for the last three terms, we use Lemma
2.1
and
3.1
to obtain

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\partial}\mu_{t}(v\cdot B\cdot v)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\partial}\mu_{t}(v\cdot n^{\perp})n^{\perp}\cdot B\cdot v\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int\mu_{t}\nabla^{\perp}(v\cdot n^{\perp})\cdot B\cdot v+\int v\cdot n^{\perp}\nabla^{\perp}\cdot[\mu_{t}B\cdot v]\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1}}\left(\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|v\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\right)\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C_{\varepsilon_{1}}\left\|v\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\varepsilon_{1}\left(\left\|\nabla\rho_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|v\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\left|\int\mu_{t}\left|\curle v\right|^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon_{2}}\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{2}\left\|v\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|\int\mu^{\prime}\nabla^{\perp}\rho\cdot v_{t}(\curle v)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon_{3}}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{3}\left(\left\|v_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|v\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\right),

which gives

(3.51)

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{d}{dt}\left(\mathcal{M}_{1}(t)+\|\sqrt{\mu}\curle v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle-C_{\varepsilon_{4}}\left(\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|v\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}+\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\right)\left(\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+C_{\varepsilon_{4}}\left\|v\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{4}\left(\left\|\nabla\rho_{t}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left\|v_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|v\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\right).

On the other hand,

(3.52)
